Hybrid Pipe Dreams for Key Polynomials
Yihan Xiao, Rui Xiong, Haofeng Zhang

Abstract
. We develop a family of new combinatorial models for key polynomials. It is similar to the hybrid pipe dream model for Schubert polynomials defined recently by Knutson and Udell.



1.
Introduction

The key polynomials
\kappa_{\alpha}(x)
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
are the Demazure characters for general linear groups
GL_{n}
.
They are a non-symmetric generalization of the Schur polynomials
s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, the Weyl characters for
GL_{n}
.
Thanks to Demazure
[
11
,
12
]
, the key polynomials are characterized via Demazure operators
\pi_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq n
) on the polynomial ring
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
by

\pi_{i}f=\frac{x_{i}\cdot f-x_{i+1}\cdot f|_{x_{i}\leftrightarrow x_{i+1}}}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}.

Precisely, when
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
is a partition, i.e.,
\alpha_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\alpha_{n}
, the key polynomial is a monomial

\kappa_{\alpha}(x)=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}.

Otherwise, if
\alpha_{i}<\alpha_{i+1}
, we have

\kappa_{\alpha}=\pi_{i}\kappa_{\alpha^{\prime}}

where
\alpha^{\prime}
is the composition obtained from
\alpha
by exchanging
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{i+1}
.

The characterization above determines key polynomials algebraically, while combinatorial formulas are needed for computation and study their properties.
The first combinatorial object for key polynomials is due to Lascoux and Schützenberger
[
27
]
by a generalization of semi-standard Young tableaux.
After that, many different combinatorial models were introduced

[
24
,
43
,
4
,
5
,
7
,
8
]
by
Kohnert, Reiner, Shimozono, Assaf, Quijad Searles, Busiumas, Scrimshaw, etc.
For general Lie types, combinatorial models of Demazure characters were studied in

[
33
,
34
,
23
,
31
,
32
]
by Littlemann, Kashiwara, Lenart, Postinkov, etc.

On the other hand, Macdonald
[
35
]

introduced a family of symmetric functions
P_{\lambda}
with two variables
q
,
t
, see Macdonald’s book
[
36
, chapter VI]
.
By (4.14) loc. cit., they specialize to Schur functions by setting
q=t
.
The non-symmetric generalization
E_{\alpha}(x;q,t)
was developed
by Opdam
[
42
]
, Macdonald
[
37
]
and Cherednik
[
13
]
.
It was first pointed out by Ion
[
22
]
that

\kappa_{\alpha}(x)=E_{\alpha}(x;q=\infty,t=\infty).

Haglund, Haiman and Loehr
[
21
]
gave the first combinatorial model of
E_{\alpha}
in terms of non-attacking skyline fillings.
In particular, it gives a new combinatorial model for key polynomials, studied by
[
39
,
40
,
4
]
by Monical, Monical, Pechenik, Searles, Assaf, etc.

There are many other combinatorial models of
E_{\alpha}
, see
[
44
,
10
,
1
,
30
,
3
]
by Ram, Yip, Borodin, Wheeler, Aggarwal, Lenart, Alexandersson, etc.
Each of them should specialize a combinatorial model of key polynomials.
The model we are going to specialize in this paper is from Borodin and Wheeler
[
10
]
via vertex models.
We remark that in the proof of loc. cit., we have to keep two variables
q,t
unspecialized, so we do not have a good explanation why this model satisfies the algebraic recursion given by Demazure operators.
Besides, the result of Busiumas and Scrimshaw
[
8
]
implies another vertex model for key polynomials, which agrees with the one specialized from
[
1
]
.

Schur polynomials admit another family of non-symmetric generalization known as Schubert polynomials
\mathfrak{S}_{w}
for permutations
w
.
Schubert polynomials were also introduced by Lascoux and Schützenberger
[
29
]
to study the intersection theory over flag varieties.
A modern treatment of this topic can be found in the book by Anderson and Fulton
[
2
]
.
They satisfy a similar recursion formula as key polynomials, using a different family of operators.
Based on Fomin and Stanley
[
18
]
and Fomin and Kirillov
[
16
]
, Bergeron and Billey
[
9
]
defined a combinatorial model for Schubert polynomials, now known as pipe dreams (PD).
Many years after PD was found, Lam, Lee and Shimozono
[
26
]
discovered a different combinatorial model for Schubert polynomials called bumpless pipe dreams (BPD).
Although the two models compute the same polynomial, it is far from obvious that there exists a weight-preserving bijection between them.
A “canonical” bijection was established by Gao and Huang
[
19
]
.

Recently, a new approach was introduced by Knutson and Udell
[
25
]
.
They gave a new family of models called hybrid pipe dreams (HPD) for Schubert polynomials including both PD and BPD.
In their model, any two of HPD models are connected by a path of HPD models with explicit local weight-preserving bijections at each step.
In particular, it gives a bijection between PD and BPD inductively.

In this paper, we develop the hybrid version for key polynomials (Theorem
2.2
).
It generalizes the model specialized from
[
10
]
.
As an application, we derive a two-side branching rule for key polynomials (Corollary
2.4
).

Let us briefly discuss our approach.
First, we take the model specialized from Borodin and Wheeler
[
10
]
as an initial model, which should be viewed as an analogy of PD for Schubert polynomials.
Then, by introducing another family of pieces, we can define our hybrid pipe dream model.
Lastly, we find an explicit weight-preserving bijection between different models, similar to Knutson and Udell
[
25
, §2]
.
The most essential step in the proof is the case of exchanging two adjacent rows of different types.

Let us finish the introduction by discuss some further questions.
It is natural to ask whether our model can be extended to non-symmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{\alpha}
.
But the weights for
E_{\alpha}
in Borodin and Wheeler
[
10
]
are quite complicated, and we do not have a good guess for its hybrid version.

It was noticed by Lascoux and Schützenberger
[
28
]
that
Schubert polynomials expand positively into key polynomials.
A proof can be found in Reiner and Shimozono
[
43
]
,
see also Assaf and Schilling
[
6
]
, Shimozono and Yu
[
38
]
and Gold, Milićević and Sun
[
20
]
.
It is natural to ask whether such relations can be seen from HPD models for Schubert polynomials and key polynomials.

Recently, Yu
[
45
, Theorem 6.7]
gave a bijection between HPD for Schubert polynomials to Bruhat chains.
For key polynomials, a chain formula is also expected. Our model should give some clue in this direction.

There are many conjectures in
[
41
]
concerning key polynomials.
Some of them were confirmed recently in
[
17
,
14
,
15
]
by Fink, Mészáros, Dizier, Fan, Guo, Peng, Sun, etc.
Since our model has the feature that the rows contributing
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
can be adjacent for any not necessarily adjacent indices
i\neq j
, it possibly provides a way of approaching these conjectures.

The paper is organized as follows.
In section
2.1
and
2.2
we introduce our model and state the main theorem (Theorem
2.2
).
In section
2.3
we discuss the two-side branching rule (Corollary
2.4
).
In section
2.4
, we identify the model specialized from Borodin and Wheeler
[
10
]
with one of our model, and reduce the proof of Theorem
2.2
to the case of swapping two adjacent rows.
Section
3
will be devoted to establish the swapping.
For this purpose, we need to distinguish two types of sub-columns, and they are dealt separately in section
3.1
and
3.2
.

2.
Pipe dream model

2.1.
The tiles

In this paper, we will fix an integer
n\geq 1
.
we will denote
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and take it as the set of colors.
Our pipe dream model consists of two types of tiles which can be decomposed further into smaller sub-tiles.

A
west tile
is a horizontal stack of
n
sub-tiles

12.0pt\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{14.39996pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[14.29997pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[-0.09999pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}}}\makebox[12.0pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[6.59999pt]{12.0pt}{1.19998pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{1.19998pt}{14.39996pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}}\end{array}}\qquad{\begin{array}[]{l}\raisebox{0.0pt}[14.39996pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{14.39996pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hskip with each “pipe” colored by a number from
[n]
.
We require the following conditions on the west tiles

•

only the pipe colored by
i\in[n]
can touch the
i
-th floor or the
i
-th ceiling;

•

in the sub-tile
{\begin{array}[]{l}\raisebox{0.0pt}[15.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hskip 12.0pt\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[14.90001pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[-0.09999pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}}}\makebox[12.0pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[6.90001pt]{12.0pt}{1.19998pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{1.19998pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}}\end{array}}
, the horizontal pipe is colored less than the vertical pipe.

In particular, a pipe can only turn at most once in a tile.

Similarly, an
east tile
is a horizontal stack of
n
sub-tiles

with each “pipe” colored by a number from
[n]
.
We require the following conditions on the east tiles

•

only the pipe colored by
i\in[n]
can touch the
i
-th floor or the
i
-th ceiling;

•

in the sub-tile
{\begin{array}[]{l}\raisebox{0.0pt}[15.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hskip 12.0pt\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[14.90001pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[-0.09999pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}}}\makebox[12.0pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[6.90001pt]{12.0pt}{1.19998pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{1.19998pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}}\end{array}}
, the horizontal pipe is colored larger than the vertical pipe.

Note that conditions on the crossing of pipes are different.

Example 2.1
.

When
n=5
, here are some examples of west tiles.

2.2.
Main theorem

Let
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
be a composition.
We will fix an integer
N
such that
\alpha_{i}\leq N
for all
i
.
We will make a choice of type

\tau=(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n})
with
\tau_{i}\in\{W,E\}
.

Consider a grid of
n\times(N+1)
as follows:

(1)

{\begin{array}[]{l}{\raisebox{0.0pt}[15.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\rule{-0.4pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.79999pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.4pt}{0.0pt}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\hskip 7.20007pt\rule{-0.025pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.04999pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.025pt}{0.0pt}\hskip 7.20007pt\rule{-0.025pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.04999pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.025pt}{0.0pt}\hskip We refer to the leftmost column as the
0
-th column, and the next one as the
1
-st column, etc. So the rightmost column is the
N
-th column.
We refer to the top row as the first row, and the next one as the second row, etc. So the bottom row is the
n
-th row.
We say the
i
-th row is of west (resp., east) type, if
\tau_{i}
is
W
(resp.,
E
).

Next, we label the colors
[n]
on the boundary of the grid.
Let
k
be the number of rows of west type.
On the left (resp., right) boundary, we label those rows of west (resp., east) type by colors
1,2,\cdots,k
(resp.,
n,n-1,\cdots,k+1
), from up to down.
On the upper boundary, we label the color
i\in[n]
on the
i
-th ceiling of the
\alpha_{i}
-th column.
We left the lower boundary empty.

We refer the row labeled by
i
above by the
i
-row.
Namely, if
i\leq k
(resp.,
i>k
), then the
i
-row is the
i
-th (resp.,
(n+1-i)
-th) row of west (resp., east) type.
Note that the
i
-row is NOT the
i
-th row.

Let us denote by

\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)

the set of tilings of the grid, with the rows of west (resp. east) type tiled by west (resp., east) tiles.
For each
\pi\in\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)
, we define its weight

(2)

\operatorname{wt}(\pi)=x_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{m_{n}},

where
m_{i}
is the number of tiles in the
i
-row with a pipe touching (resp., not touching) the right wall if the
i
-row is of west (resp., east) type.

The main theorem of this paper is the following.

Theorem 2.2
.

For any choice of
\tau\in\{W,E\}^{n}
, we have

\kappa_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)}\operatorname{wt}(\pi).

Example 2.3
.

For example, if
n=4
and
\alpha=(1,3,0,2)
.
we can take
N=3
and
\tau=WEEW
.
Consider the following element in
\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)

(we draw a dot on the walls contributing weights)

Its weight is
x_{1}^{3}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}
.
The following table might be helpful:

\begin{array}[]{ccccl}\hline\cr\text{$i$-row}&\text{descrption}&\text{type}&m_{i}&\hfill\text{explanation}\hfill\\
\hline\cr\text{$1$-row}&\text{the $1$-st row}&W&3&\text{since pipe $1$ intersects once and pipe $2$ intersects twice}\\
\text{$2$-row}&\text{the $4$-th row}&W&2&\text{since pipe $2$ intersects twice}\\
\text{$3$-row}&\text{the $3$-rd row}&E&1&\text{since there is one wall without pipe intersected}\\
\text{$4$-row}&\text{the $2$-nd row}&E&0&\text{since all walls are intersected}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Besides this, there rest
12
elements in
\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)
, see
Figure
1
.
The key polynomial is exactly the sum of their weights.

the rest
12
elements in
\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)

2.3.
Branching rule

As an application of Theorem
2.2
, let us give a two-side branching rule for key polynomials.
Let
0\leq a<a+m\leq n
.
We can give a combinatorial formula of the expansion

\kappa_{\alpha}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{\beta}c_{\alpha\beta}^{(a)}\cdot\kappa_{\beta}(x_{a+1},\ldots,x_{a+m})

where the sum over compositions
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m}
and

c_{\alpha\beta}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{a},x_{a+m+1},\ldots,x_{n}].

For this purpose, we need slightly extend our model.
Assume we are given two compositions

\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
and

\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m}
.
We pick an
N
such that
\alpha_{i}\leq N
and
\beta_{i}\leq N
for all
i
.
We fix a choice of
\tau\in\{W,E\}^{n-m}
with
a
many
W
’s.
Consider a grid of
(n-m)\times(N+1)
as in (
1
).
We can repeat what we did in section
2.1
, except now we also label the color
i+a\in[n]
on the
i
-th floor of the
\beta_{i}
-th column.
We will denote the set of tilings by
\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha/\beta)
.
For any
\pi\in\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha/\beta)
, we define its weight
\operatorname{wt}(\pi)
by the same formula as in (
2
).

We have a simple bijection between

\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha/\beta)\times\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau^{\prime}}(\beta)\cong\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau\oplus\tau^{\prime}}(\alpha)

by stacking two tilings.
By Theorem
2.2
, it follows immediately that

Corollary 2.4
.

For any choice of
\tau\in\{W,E\}^{n-m}
with
a
many
W
’s,
we have

c_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha/\beta)}\operatorname{wt}(\pi).

Example 2.5
.

Let us consider
\alpha=(1,3,0,2)
and
\beta=(3,1)
with
\tau=WE
.
Here are the elements of
\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha/\beta)
:

We see that

c_{\alpha\beta}=x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{4}
.

2.4.
Preparation for the proof

Let us discuss the proof of Theorem
2.2
.
When
\tau=W^{n}
, only the west tiles are used.
By comparing our model with the weights in Borodin and Wheeler
[
10
, (3.7)]
, we see under the notation from loc. cit.,
the weighted sum

\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)}\operatorname{wt}(\pi)=f^{n\cdots 21}_{\alpha}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q=0,t=0).

As pointed out under
[
10
, Theorem 1.3]
, the polynomial
f^{n\cdots 21}_{\alpha}
is the permuted basement non-symmetric Macdonald polynomial introduced by Alexandersson
[
3
]
.
The specialization
q=t=0
gives key polynomials, see the table under
[
3
, Question 7]
.

Therefore, it reduces to finding a weight-preserving bijection among HPDs for different
\tau
’s.
Following the idea of Knutson and Udell
[
25
]
, it suffices to solve two basic cases:

(a)

changing the type of the bottom row;

(b)

swapping two adjacent rows of different types.

The case (
a
) is easy, simply by flipping the unique pipe in the bottom row:

Figure 2
.
The cases (
a
) and (
b
)

3.
Swapping two rows

In this section, we will establish the case (
b
). The bijection is constructed at the sub-tile level.
Namely, we will cut each column into
n
sub-columns, resulting
(N+1)n
sub-columns.
Let
\pi\in\operatorname{HPD}_{\tau}(\alpha)
.
Let us first state an equivalent way of computing weight
\operatorname{wt}(\pi)
.
We define the weight of a west (resp., east) sub-tile with a pipe touching (resp., not touching) the right wall, i.e.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c}\text{west}&\text{east}\\

to be
x_{i}
if it is in the
i
-row and in the last sub-columns of a column.
The weights of all other sub-tiles are
1
.
Then the weight
\operatorname{wt}(\pi)
is the product of the weights of sub-tiles in
\pi
.

12.0pt\rule{-0.025pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.04999pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.025pt}{0.0pt}}\rule{-0.4pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.79999pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.4pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-60.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[14.6pt]{60.0pt}{0.79999pt}\rule{-60.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[-0.4pt]{60.0pt}{0.79999pt}}}\makebox[60.0pt][l]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\rule{30.0pt}{0.0pt}\raisebox{3.75pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\displaystyle\cdots$}}}}}\makebox[12.0pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\raisebox{3.75pt}[15.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\displaystyle b$}}}\end{array}}

labeled by colors
a<b
respectively.
Note all the pipes appearing in the two rows are those pipes colored by
\{a,\ldots,b\}
.
Namely, the lower boundary is labeled by colors
\{a+1,\ldots,b-1\}
and the upper boundary is labeled by colors
\{a,\ldots,b\}
.
For a given boundary condition, we are going to establish a weight preserving bijection between type
WE
and the corresponding two rows of type
EW
:

To do this, we need more notations.
For
a\leq c\leq b
, we define the source
s(c)
(resp., the target
t(c)
) to be the sub-column colored by
c
on the lower (resp., upper) boundary.
Here we take the convention that define
s(a)=-\infty
and
s(b)=\infty
.

We say a sub-column is
frozen
if it is between sub-columns
s(c)
and
t(c)
for some
a\leq c\leq b
(not including
s(c)
and
t(c)
).
Otherwise we say a sub-column is
unfrozen
.
We say a unfrozen sub-column is
critical
if
s(c)
or
t(c)
for some
a\leq c\leq b
.

Example 3.1
.

Consider the following example when
n=7
.
The following is a tiling of type
WE
.

We label the frozen sub-columns (by “
-
”) and the critical sub-columns (by “
\times
”) on it.
For readers who are eager to gain an insight into the bijection we will establish, we recommend referring directly to Example
3.3
later.

3.1.
Frozen sub-columns

Let us first deal with a single frozen sub-column.
Assume it is between
s(c)
and
t(c)
for
a\leq c\leq b
.
Since in rows of different types the pipe goes along different directions, the pipe colored by
c
must appear in the frozen sub-column and can only go horizontally.

For type
WE
, a frozen sub-column must be one of:

(3)

(d>c)&(c>e)&(f\neq c)&\end{array}

Let us explain the condition of
d>c
, the condition for
c>e
is similar.
In the first and the second cases, we have to assume
d>c
by our assumption on
{\begin{array}[]{l}\raisebox{0.0pt}[15.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hskip 12.0pt\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{0.2pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[14.90001pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}\rule{-12.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[-0.09999pt]{12.0pt}{0.2pt}}}\makebox[12.0pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule[6.90001pt]{12.0pt}{1.19998pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\rule{-6.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}\rule[0.0pt]{1.19998pt}{15.0pt}\rule{-0.59999pt}{0.0pt}}\end{array}}
.
In the third case, the pipe colored by
c
and
d
will finally intersect, i.e. we will meet

(d>c)&(c>e)&(f\neq c)&\end{array}

In a frozen sub-column, we define a bijection
by sending sub-columns in (
3
) to those in (
4
) respectively.
Note that colors on the left and right boundaries are swapped.
It is straightforward to see this is a weight-preserving bijection.

3.2.
Unfrozen sub-columns

We still need to deal with unfrozen sub-columns.
We say a critical sub-column is of type
M
(resp.,
L
,
R
) if both of its left and its right (resp., only its left, only its right) are unfrozen.

Let us consider a maximal interval
I
of unfrozen sub-columns, i.e. it is the collection of unfrozen sub-columns between two critical sub-columns of type
L
and
R
(including both critical sub-columns).
The critical sub-columns
m_{0},m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
(from left to right) in
I
cut the interval into sub-intervals
I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}
(from left to right).
We show them in a diagram

Let
N_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,k
) be the maximal integer such that

nN_{i}\leq|I_{i}|
.
We will construct a bijection between
tilings of the interval
I
of type
WE
(resp.,
EW
) and the set

(5)

\bigg\{(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}):0\leq j_{i}\leq N_{i}\text{ for $i=1,\cdots,k$}\bigg\}.

Let us first deal with the type
WE
.
Let
1\leq i\leq k
.
In the sub-columns
I_{i}\cup\{m_{i}\}
, there must exists a sub-column
m
such that the tiling is

Let us define
j_{i}=\dfrac{m_{i}-m}{n}
.
Since
m\in I_{i}\cup\{m_{i}\}
, we have
0\leq j_{i}\leq N_{i}
.
This defines a map to (
5
).
Note that each
j_{i}
determines the tiling in the interval
I_{i}\cup\{m_{i}\}
. Moreover on
m_{0}
, the tiling must be

We define
j_{i}=\dfrac{m-m_{i-1}}{n}
.
Similarly as the
WE
cases, this defines a bijection to (
5
).

\makebox[8.39996pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\displaystyle\times$}}}\makebox[8.39996pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}}\makebox[8.39996pt]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[41.99982pt][l]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}}}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[41.99982pt][l]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle\times$}}}}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[41.99982pt][l]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}}}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[41.99982pt][l]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}}}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[41.99982pt][l]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle\times$}}}}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[41.99982pt][l]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}}}\raisebox{6.0pt}[24.0pt]{\makebox[41.99982pt][l]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\makebox[8.39996pt]{$\displaystyle\times$}}}}\end{array}}

Now we get a bijection
between the tilings of the interval
I
of type
WE
and of type
EW
.
From the discussion above, it is direct to see the colors on the boundary of
I
are swapped.
Let
w
be the number of sub-columns in
I\setminus\{m_{k}\}
appearing as the last sub-columns in a column.
Then it is clear that the weight of a tiling is

x_{a}^{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}}x_{b}^{w-j_{1}-\cdots-j_{k}}

for both
WE
and
EW
.
This shows the bijection is weight-preserving.

Example 3.3
.

Let us apply our bijection to Example
3.1
.

Combining the two bijections for frozen and unfrozen sub-columns together, we get a bijection of two adjacent rows of type
WE
and type
EW
.
The case (
b
) is established.
